Becoming a strategic reader. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 8(3), 293–316. https://doi.org/10.1016/0361-476X(83)90018-8**: The source paper for the declarative-procedural-conditional taxonomy as applied to metacognitive knowledge and reading strategy instruction. Essential for understanding the conceptual foundations of the DPC framework and its original application to reading strategy development.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125–173. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0079-7421(08)60053-5**: The foundational paper for the monitoring-control hierarchy. Technically dense but provides the most precise account available of how metacognitive processes interact with object-level cognition. The framework presented here has been enormously generative for experimental research on memory monitoring and control.

**Recommended Sections:** Pages 125–145 for the theoretical framework; the remainder for empirical applications.

- **Schraw, G., & Moshman, D. (1995). Metacognitive theories. *Educational Psychology Review*, 7(4), 351–371. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02212307**: The best single theoretical synthesis of metacognitive frameworks available in the literature. Schraw and Moshman organize the field's competing accounts into a coherent comparative framework, clarifying the relationships among the major theories while identifying their points of genuine disagreement.

- **Schraw, G., & Dennison, R. S. (1994). Assessing metacognitive awareness. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 19(4), 460–475. https://doi.org/10.1006/ceps.1994.1033**: The source paper for the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory (MAI), the most widely used self-report instrument in metacognitive knowledge research. Essential for understanding the operationalization of metacognitive knowledge and for critically evaluating the self-report measurement approach.

- **Efklides, A. (2006). Metacognition and affect: What can metacognitive experiences tell us about the learning process? *Educational Research Review*, 1(1), 3–14. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.edurev.2005.11.001**: A highly accessible overview of Efklides's framework on metacognitive experiences and their role in self-regulated learning. The best entry point into the phenomenological dimension of metacognitive processing.

- **Efklides, A. (2011). Interactions of metacognition with motivation and affect in self-regulated learning: The MASRL model. *Educational Psychologist*, 46(1), 6–25. https://doi.org/10.1080/00461520.2011.538645**: Efklides's most comprehensive theoretical synthesis, presenting the Metacognitive and Affective Model of Self-Regulated Learning (MASRL). More technical than the 2006 paper but provides a fully integrated account of how metacognitive feelings, metacognitive knowledge, and affective states interact in the self-regulation of learning.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2002). The role of metacognitive knowledge in learning, teaching, and assessing. *Theory Into Practice*, 41(4), 219–225. https://doi.org/10.1207/s15430421tip4104_3**: Pintrich's accessible treatment of metacognitive knowledge within the SRL framework. Particularly valuable for its treatment of the four types of knowledge (factual, conceptual, procedural, metacognitive) and their roles in self-regulated learning.

- **Koriat, A., & Bjork, R. A. (2005). Illusions of competence in monitoring one's knowledge during study. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition*, 31(2), 187–194.**: Key empirical paper on the miscalibration of judgments of learning and the mechanisms through which learners overestimate their own knowledge during study. Foundational for the calibration literature reviewed in Phase IV.5.

- **Alexander, P. A. (1995). Superimposing a situation-specific and domain-specific perspective on an account of self-regulated learning. *Educational Psychologist*, 30(4), 189–193.**: A critical counterpoint to domain-general accounts of metacognitive knowledge. Alexander's domain-specific perspective informs the tension discussed in Appendix Section 3.

- **Hattie, J., & Timperley, H. (2007). The power of feedback. *Review of Educational Research*, 77(1), 81–112. https://doi.org/10.3102/003465430298487**: This review of feedback research is relevant to metacognitive knowledge development through its documentation of how feedback supports calibration. The Hattie-Timperley model's four levels of feedback (task, process, self-regulation, self) map directly onto the dimensions of metacognitive knowledge addressed in this report.

**Recommended Sections:** Pages 90–101 on feedback and self-regulation.

- **Dent, A. L., & Koenka, A. C. (2016). The relation between self-regulated learning and academic achievement across childhood and adolescence: A meta-analysis. *Educational Psychology Review*, 28(3), 425–474.**: Meta-analytic evidence for the relationship between self-regulated learning (including metacognitive knowledge components) and academic achievement. Provides quantitative estimates of effect sizes referenced in Phase III.5.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist, 34*(10), 906–911.**: The originating theoretical paper that introduced metacognition as a research domain and established the knowledge-experiences-regulation tripartite framework. Essential reading for any serious treatment of metacognitive regulation, as it provides the terminological and conceptual foundation for the entire subsequent literature. Particularly important for Flavell's analysis of metacognitive experiences as the phenomenological bridge between knowledge and regulation.

**Recommended Sections:** Full paper; particularly sections on metacognitive knowledge taxonomy and metacognitive monitoring examples.

- **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. Weinert & R. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, Motivation, and Understanding* (pp. 65–116). Erlbaum.**: Brown's most comprehensive theoretical treatment of metacognitive regulation as the executive dimension of cognition. Provides the clearest account of the knowledge-regulation distinction, the production deficiency phenomenon in children, and the empirical rationale for treating regulation as a distinct object of research and instruction.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections on regulation of cognition; production vs. mediation deficiency; metacognitive training.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation, 26*, 125–173.**: The foundational paper for the formal monitoring-control model that is the most architecturally precise account of metacognitive regulation. Introduces the meta-level/object-level distinction, the monitoring-control direction specificity, and the taxonomy of metacognitive judgment types (JOL, FOK, EOL). This paper is indispensable for understanding the formal structure of metacognitive regulation.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections 2-4 on theoretical framework; the judgment taxonomy; empirical studies on JOL accuracy.

- **Schraw, G., & Moshman, D. (1995). Metacognitive theories. *Educational Psychology Review, 7*(4), 351–371.**: The most widely cited synthesis paper in the educational metacognition literature. Provides the planning-monitoring-evaluating taxonomy and the declarative-procedural-conditional knowledge taxonomy that organize most subsequent educational research on metacognition. Essential for understanding how the Nelson-Narens framework was translated into educational psychology.

**Recommended Sections:** Full paper; particularly the regulatory component taxonomy and the evidence for metacognitive training effects.

- **Efklides, A. (2011). Interactions of metacognition with motivation and affect in self-regulated learning: The MASRL model. *Educational Psychologist, 46*(1), 6–25.**: Efklides' most comprehensive presentation of the MASRL model, integrating metacognitive experiences with motivational and affective dimensions of SRL. Provides the theoretical basis for the Calibration-Action Gap's motivational interruption mechanism and for the claim that metacognitive regulation cannot be adequately understood in purely cognitive terms.

**Recommended Sections:** Full paper; particularly the MASRL model diagram and the discussion of metacognitive experiences as mediating variables.

- **Koriat, A. (1997). Monitoring one's own knowledge during study: A cue-utilization approach to judgments of learning. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General, 126*(4), 349–370.**: Introduces the cue-utilization framework for understanding JOL construction, demonstrating that JOLs are based on available cues (intrinsic, extrinsic, mnemonic) rather than direct access to memory traces, and that mnemonic cues — particularly fluency — carry excessive weight. Essential for understanding why monitoring is systematically distorted in specific ways.

**Recommended Sections:** Cue taxonomy sections; experiments demonstrating fluency effects on JOL accuracy.

- **Winne, P. H., & Hadwin, A. F. (1998). Studying as self-regulated engagement in learning. In D. Hacker, J. Dunlosky, & A. Graesser (Eds.), *Metacognition in Educational Theory and Practice* (pp. 277–304). Erlbaum.**: The foundational presentation of the COPES model, providing the most computationally elaborate account of metacognitive regulation as distributed within learning episodes. Essential for understanding how regulatory processes are embedded within SRL cycles rather than operating as supervisory overlays.

**Recommended Sections:** The COPES framework sections; the four-phase model of studying; the role of standards in regulatory comparison.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). Academic Press.**: Pintrich's integrative SRL framework paper, providing the 4×4 matrix of regulatory areas (cognitive, motivational, behavioral, contextual) by phases (planning, monitoring, control, reflection) that situates metacognitive regulation within a motivational context. Essential for understanding why purely cognitive accounts of metacognitive regulation are incomplete.

**Recommended Sections:** The 4×4 matrix sections; integration of motivational and metacognitive regulation.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's comprehensive account of his cyclical SRL model, situating metacognitive regulation across the three phases of the SRL cycle. Provides the most widely adopted temporal architecture for understanding how metacognitive regulation unfolds longitudinally across learning episodes.

**Recommended Sections:** Cyclical model sections; phase-specific regulatory processes; forethought-performance-self-reflection descriptions.

- **Dunlosky, J., & Metcalfe, J. (2009). *Metacognition*. SAGE Publications.**: The most comprehensive single-volume treatment of metacognition research, integrating the metamemory tradition with the SRL and educational psychology traditions. Provides detailed coverage of all the judgment types (JOL, FOK, EOL, confidence), the calibration literature, and implications for educational practice.

**Recommended Sections:** Chapters 3-5 on monitoring judgments; Chapters 7-8 on control processes; Chapter 10 on metacognition in educational contexts.

- **Schraw, G., & Dennison, R. S. (1994). Assessing metacognitive awareness. *Contemporary Educational Psychology, 19*(4), 460–475.**: Development and validation paper for the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory, the most widely used self-report measure of metacognitive regulation in educational research. Essential for understanding how metacognitive regulation is operationalized and measured in large-sample studies, and for understanding the measurement limitations that qualify the interpretation of intervention research.

**Recommended Sections:** Scale development sections; factor analysis demonstrating knowledge-regulation distinction; reliability and validity evidence.

- **Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about Knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: Bjork's influential treatment of desirable difficulties in learning, demonstrating that the conditions that improve long-term retention (spacing, interleaving, retrieval practice) reduce subjective fluency and thereby distort metacognitive judgments toward underconfidence in the efficacy of effective strategies. Essential for understanding the practical implications of the fluency illusion.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections on desirable difficulties; the disconnect between subjective ease and actual learning.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction, 1*(2), 117–175.**: The foundational reciprocal teaching paper, providing empirical evidence for the effectiveness of metacognitive scaffolding in reading comprehension. Documents the development and empirical validation of the reciprocal teaching method and provides the most robust evidence base for metacognitive regulation instruction in educational contexts.

**Recommended Sections:** Full paper; study 2 main findings; discussion of metacognitive scaffolding and internalization.

- **Dunlosky, J., & Nelson, T. O. (1992). Importance of the kind of cue for judgments of learning (JOL) and the delayed-JOL effect. *Memory & Cognition*, 20(4), 374–380.**: Introduced the monitoring dual memories hypothesis, explaining the delayed JOL effect as a shift from short-term to long-term memory cues. Essential reading for understanding the cue-mediated nature of monitoring. Focus on the MDM hypothesis in the discussion section.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The foundational article that defined metacognition as a distinct psychological construct and introduced the taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy variables). Essential historical context for the Nelson–Narrens model. The entire article is relevant; it is brief and highly cited.

- **Fleming, S. M., & Lau, H. C. (2014). How to measure metacognition. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*, 8, 443.**: Introduced the meta-d' framework for measuring metacognitive sensitivity using signal detection theory. Provides the formal measurement complement to the Nelson–Narrens architectural framework. Focus on the derivation and interpretation of meta-d'.

- **Hart, J. T. (1965). Memory and the feeling-of-knowing experience. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 56(4), 208–216.**: The first systematic experimental study of feeling-of-knowing, demonstrating that FOK judgments predict subsequent recognition performance at above-chance levels. Establishes the empirical foundation for the FOK component of the Nelson–Narrens taxonomy.

- **Koriat, A. (1997). Monitoring one's own knowledge during study: A cue-utilization approach to judgments of learning. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 126(4), 349–370.**: The definitive statement of the cue utilisation approach to metacognitive monitoring. Distinguishes intrinsic, extrinsic, and mnemonic cues and demonstrates their differential influence on JOL accuracy. The key complement to the Nelson–Narrens architectural framework.

- **Metcalfe, J. (2002). Is study time allocated selectively to a region of proximal learning? *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 131(3), 349–363.**: Proposes and tests the region of proximal learning hypothesis, showing that learners allocate study time to items of intermediate difficulty rather than to the most difficult items. Refines the Nelson–Narrens control channel with a normative optimality model.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Dunlosky, J. (1991). When people's judgments of learning (JOLs) are extremely accurate at predicting subsequent recall: The "delayed-JOL effect." *Psychological Science*, 2(4), 267–270.**: Reports the delayed JOL effect — the finding that JOLs made after a delay are dramatically more accurate than immediate JOLs. One of the most robust findings in the metacognition literature and a cornerstone of the cue-mediated monitoring account.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. H. Bower (Ed.), *The psychology of learning and motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125–173). Academic Press.**: The canonical statement of the Nelson–Narrens metacognitive model. Introduces the meta-level/object-level architecture, specifies the monitoring and control channels, and presents the taxonomy of metacognitive judgments. The primary reference for this report.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1994). Why investigate metacognition? In J. Metcalfe & A. P. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about knowing* (pp. 1–25). MIT Press.**: Provides the philosophical and theoretical justification for the metacognition research programme, situating the monitoring–control model within broader debates about the nature of self-knowledge. Recommended sections: the arguments for treating metacognition as an informational relation rather than a phenomenological capacity.

- **Schraw, G., & Dennison, R. S. (1994). Assessing metacognitive awareness. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 19(4), 460–475.**: Introduces the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory (MAI), operationalising the knowledge-of-cognition and regulation-of-cognition distinction for educational assessment. Important for understanding how the Nelson–Narrens framework has been translated into practical measurement.

- **Fleming, S. M. (2021). *Know Thyself: The Science of Self-Awareness*. Basic Books.**: A comprehensive, accessible synthesis of metacognition research from the neuroscientific perspective. Provides an excellent overview of how the Nelson–Narrens framework connects to neural substrates, signal detection approaches, and computational models.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of Reading Research*. New York: Longman.**: Foundational treatment of comprehension monitoring and fix-up strategies during reading. Directly supports the analysis in Section III of monitoring mechanisms and their failure modes.

- **Bjork, R. A., Dunlosky, J., & Kornell, N. (2013). Self-regulated learning: Beliefs, techniques, and illusions. *Annual Review of Psychology*, 64, 417-444.**: Comprehensive review of metacognitive illusions — fluency illusion, overconfidence, desirable difficulties — that informed the calibration analysis in Section V. Key source for the argument that subjective learning signals are systematically misleading.

- **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. E. Weinert & R. H. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, Motivation, and Understanding* (pp. 65-116). Hillsdale, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum.**: Established the knowledge-of-cognition vs. regulation-of-cognition distinction that structures the entire report. Essential reading for understanding the dual nature of metacognition.

- **Clark, A., & Chalmers, D. J. (1998). The extended mind. *Analysis*, 58(1), 7-19.**: The foundational paper for the extended mind thesis, providing the philosophical framework for the Extended Metacognitive Architecture Model (EMAM) developed in Section VI. The parity principle is the key argumentative tool.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906-911.**: The seminal paper introducing metacognition as a research programme. Establishes the taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy) and introduces the concept of metacognitive experiences. Starting point for Section I.

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Unskilled and unaware of it: How difficulties in recognizing one's own incompetence lead to inflated self-assessments. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 77(6), 1121-1134.**: Original paper on the Dunning-Kruger effect. Supports the calibration analysis in Section V regarding the structural bootstrapping problem of metacognitive assessment.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. H. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125-173). New York: Academic Press.**: The paper that established the meta-level/object-level model of metacognitive monitoring and control. Foundational for the monitoring-control architecture analysed throughout the report.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science*, 17(3), 249-255.**: Influential demonstration of the testing effect. Supports the argument in Sections III and V that retrieval practice provides more accurate metacognitive monitoring signals than re-reading.

- **Schraw, G. (1998). Promoting general metacognitive awareness. *Instructional Science*, 26, 113-125.**: Synthesised the tripartite model of metacognitive knowledge (declarative, procedural, conditional) used in Section I. The conditional knowledge component is particularly important for the report's analysis of strategy selection in PKM contexts.

- **Sparrow, B., Liu, J., & Wegner, D. M. (2011). Google effects on memory: Cognitive consequences of having information at our fingertips. *Science*, 333(6043), 776-778.**: Empirical evidence for the cognitive offloading effect discussed in Section V. Demonstrates that knowledge of external storage availability reduces internal encoding effort, supporting the PKM calibration paradox analysis.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13-39). San Diego: Academic Press.**: The foundational articulation of the three-phase SRL cycle (forethought → performance → self-reflection) that provides the structural framework for the PKM-SRL isomorphism analysed in Section IV.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451-502). San Diego: Academic Press.**: Develops the four-column SRL framework integrating cognitive, motivational, behavioural, and contextual regulation. Supports the report's acknowledgment of motivational dimensions discussed in Section IV and the limitations section.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911. https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.34.10.906**: The foundational paper for the entire field. Flavell introduces the term "metacognition," provides the tripartite taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge, experiences, and regulation, and outlines the research program that would follow. Essential reading for understanding the conceptual origins of all metacognitive reading research.

**Recommended Sections:** Full text; particularly pp. 906–909 for the taxonomy.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of reading research* (pp. 353–394). Longman.**: The defining synthesis of metacognitive principles applied to reading comprehension. Baker and Brown articulate comprehension monitoring, its evaluation standards, and the fix-up strategy repertoire with a precision that subsequent research has not substantially superseded. Essential for Phases III and IV of this report.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections on evaluation standards (pp. 359–368) and regulatory responses (pp. 368–382).

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125–173. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0079-7421(08)60053-5**: Establishes the object-level/meta-level architecture and the monitoring-control framework that provides the formal computational backbone for the field. Indispensable for the mechanistic analysis in Phase IV; the monitoring-control coupling concept derives directly from this framework.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections 2 and 3 on the formal framework; the empirical work on JoL and FoK.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction*, 1(2), 117–175. https://doi.org/10.1207/s1532690xci0102_1**: The original reciprocal teaching paper. Documents the development, theoretical rationale, and initial empirical evaluation of the intervention. Required reading for Section 5.1 of this report; provides the most important single example of metacognitive reading theory instantiated in pedagogical practice.

**Recommended Sections:** The theoretical rationale section (pp. 117–124); the transcript examples throughout.

- **Schraw, G., & Moshman, D. (1995). Metacognitive theories. *Educational Psychology Review*, 7(4), 351–371. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02212307**: Provides the bipartite knowledge-regulation taxonomy and the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory. The most widely cited single account of metacognitive structure in educational psychology.

- **Kintsch, W. (1988). The role of knowledge in discourse comprehension: A construction-integration model. *Psychological Review*, 95(2), 163–182. https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-295X.95.2.163**: Establishes the Construction-Integration model of discourse comprehension, including the textbase/situation model distinction that anchors the conceptual framework in Phase II.

- **Pressley, M., & Afflerbach, P. (1995). *Verbal protocols of reading: The nature of constructively responsive reading*. Erlbaum.**: The most comprehensive empirical account of skilled reading using think-aloud protocols; documents the metacognitive strategies that expert readers actually deploy, as distinct from what they report deploying. Essential for Section 4 and for understanding the gap between self-report and behavioral evidence.

- **Gough, P. B., & Tunmer, W. E. (1986). Decoding, reading, and reading disability. *Remedial and Special Education*, 7(1), 6–10. https://doi.org/10.1177/074193258600700104**: Original paper establishing the Simple View of Reading; the foundation for Section 2.2's account of the decoding-comprehension architecture.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social-cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's systematization of his cyclic SRL model; the theoretical source for Phase III.4's treatment of self-regulated reading within the SRL framework.

- **Paris, S. G., Lipson, M. Y., & Wixson, K. K. (1983). Becoming a strategic reader. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 8(3), 293–316. https://doi.org/10.1016/0361-476X(83)90018-8**: Introduces the declarative/procedural/conditional trichotomy in the reading strategy context; the theoretical foundation for Phase III.3's treatment of the Schraw-Moshman framework.

- **Perfetti, C. A. (1985). *Reading ability*. Oxford University Press.**: The foundational text for Verbal Efficiency Theory; establishes the decoding-efficiency mechanism by which fluency constrains comprehension resources.

- **van Dijk, T. A., & Kintsch, W. (1983). *Strategies of discourse comprehension*. Academic Press.**: Original source for the situation model construct; provides the full three-level text representation architecture (surface code, textbase, situation model).

- **Duffy, G. G., Roehler, L. R., Sivan, E., Rackliffe, G., Book, C., Meloth, M. S., ... & Bassiri, D. (1987). Effects of explaining the reasoning associated with using reading strategies. *Reading Research Quarterly*, 22(3), 347–368.**: Empirical evaluation of explicit reasoning instruction for reading strategies; demonstrates that explaining *why* strategies work (conditional knowledge) is critical for transfer — a key finding for Section 5.2.

- **Rosenshine, B., & Meister, C. (1994). Reciprocal teaching: A review of the research. *Review of Educational Research*, 64(4), 479–530. https://doi.org/10.3102/00346543064004479**: Comprehensive meta-analysis of reciprocal teaching research; the primary empirical source for Section 5.1's efficacy claims.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist, 34*(10), 906–911.**: The foundational theoretical statement of metacognitive development theory. Flavell introduces the term metacognition, distinguishes metacognitive knowledge from metacognitive experiences and from metacognitive monitoring/regulation, and outlines a developmental research agenda. Essential reading for understanding the conceptual vocabulary of the field. Particularly relevant to Section 1 of this report.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation, 26*, 125–173.**: The formalization of the monitoring-control framework that structures most contemporary metacognitive research. Nelson and Narens introduce the meta-level/object-level distinction, define monitoring as upward information flow and control as downward information flow, and provide evidence for the functional independence of these two processes. Central to Sections 3 and 4 of this report.

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Unskilled and unaware of it: How difficulties in recognizing one's own incompetence lead to inflated self-assessments. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 77*(6), 1121–1134.**: The original empirical demonstration of the Dunning-Kruger pattern — the finding that lower performers show greater overconfidence bias in self-assessment across multiple competence domains. Important for its documentation of the co-developmental constraint in action. Read alongside subsequent critiques of the original statistical analysis (Nuhfer et al., 2017; Gignac & Zajenkowski, 2020) for a more nuanced picture.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction, 1*(2), 117–175.**: The original empirical demonstration of the reciprocal teaching model for reading comprehension and metacognitive strategy development. Documents large effect sizes for comprehension improvement in initially poor readers, providing the strongest direct evidence for the efficacy of metacognitive strategy instruction when properly embedded in authentic content contexts. Central to Section 8.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The clearest theoretical statement of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model, integrating forethought, performance, and self-reflection phases. Essential for understanding the relationship between metacognition and broader SRL frameworks. Relevant to Section 6.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 452–502). Academic Press.**: Pintrich's comprehensive theoretical statement of motivational-metacognitive integration within the SRL model. Particularly valuable for the account of how goal orientation (mastery vs. performance approach vs. performance avoidance) shapes metacognitive monitoring signal interpretation. Relevant to Sections 6 and 7.

- **Ericsson, K. A., Krampe, R. T., & Tesch-Römer, C. (1993). The role of deliberate practice in the acquisition of expert performance. *Psychological Review, 100*(3), 363–406.**: The foundational paper establishing deliberate practice theory; places metacognitive monitoring of performance against accurate feedback and work at the RPL as the central mechanism distinguishing expert-producing practice from mere experience accumulation. Relevant to Sections 5 and 9.

- **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. E. Weinert & R. H. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, Motivation, and Understanding* (pp. 65–116). Lawrence Erlbaum.**: Brown's comprehensive theoretical synthesis integrating metacognition, executive function, and self-regulation. Addresses the developmental trajectory across childhood and proposes a domain-general–domain-specific resolution of the transfer paradox. Relevant to Sections 2 and 8.

- **Metcalfe, J., & Finn, B. (2008). Evidence that judgments of learning are causally related to study choice. *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review, 15*(1), 174–179.**: Experimental demonstration that JOLs are not merely correlates but actual causal determinants of study allocation decisions, establishing the monitoring-to-control causal chain in metacognitive regulation. Relevant to Sections 3 and 4.

- **Fleming, S. M., & Dolan, R. J. (2012). The neural basis of metacognitive ability. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B, 367*(1594), 1338–1349.**: A key neuroscientific synthesis on the neural substrates of metacognitive monitoring, focusing on anterior PFC involvement in confidence calibration. Provides the biological underpinning for the claim that metacognitive accuracy is partly a stable individual-differences variable with developmental roots. Relevant to Sections 3 and 5.

- **Dunlosky, J., & Nelson, T. O. (1992). Importance of the kind of cue for judgments of learning (JOL) and the delayed-JOL effect. *Memory & Cognition*, 20(4), 374–380.**: Introduced the monitoring dual memories hypothesis, explaining the delayed JOL effect as a shift from short-term to long-term memory cues. Essential reading for understanding the cue-mediated nature of monitoring. Focus on the MDM hypothesis in the discussion section.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The foundational article that defined metacognition as a distinct psychological construct and introduced the taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy variables). Essential historical context for the Nelson–Narrens model. The entire article is relevant; it is brief and highly cited.

- **Fleming, S. M., & Lau, H. C. (2014). How to measure metacognition. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*, 8, 443.**: Introduced the meta-d' framework for measuring metacognitive sensitivity using signal detection theory. Provides the formal measurement complement to the Nelson–Narrens architectural framework. Focus on the derivation and interpretation of meta-d'.

- **Hart, J. T. (1965). Memory and the feeling-of-knowing experience. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 56(4), 208–216.**: The first systematic experimental study of feeling-of-knowing, demonstrating that FOK judgments predict subsequent recognition performance at above-chance levels. Establishes the empirical foundation for the FOK component of the Nelson–Narrens taxonomy.

- **Koriat, A. (1997). Monitoring one's own knowledge during study: A cue-utilization approach to judgments of learning. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 126(4), 349–370.**: The definitive statement of the cue utilisation approach to metacognitive monitoring. Distinguishes intrinsic, extrinsic, and mnemonic cues and demonstrates their differential influence on JOL accuracy. The key complement to the Nelson–Narrens architectural framework.

- **Metcalfe, J. (2002). Is study time allocated selectively to a region of proximal learning? *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 131(3), 349–363.**: Proposes and tests the region of proximal learning hypothesis, showing that learners allocate study time to items of intermediate difficulty rather than to the most difficult items. Refines the Nelson–Narrens control channel with a normative optimality model.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Dunlosky, J. (1991). When people's judgments of learning (JOLs) are extremely accurate at predicting subsequent recall: The "delayed-JOL effect." *Psychological Science*, 2(4), 267–270.**: Reports the delayed JOL effect — the finding that JOLs made after a delay are dramatically more accurate than immediate JOLs. One of the most robust findings in the metacognition literature and a cornerstone of the cue-mediated monitoring account.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. H. Bower (Ed.), *The psychology of learning and motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125–173). Academic Press.**: The canonical statement of the Nelson–Narrens metacognitive model. Introduces the meta-level/object-level architecture, specifies the monitoring and control channels, and presents the taxonomy of metacognitive judgments. The primary reference for this report.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1994). Why investigate metacognition? In J. Metcalfe & A. P. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about knowing* (pp. 1–25). MIT Press.**: Provides the philosophical and theoretical justification for the metacognition research programme, situating the monitoring–control model within broader debates about the nature of self-knowledge. Recommended sections: the arguments for treating metacognition as an informational relation rather than a phenomenological capacity.

- **Schraw, G., & Dennison, R. S. (1994). Assessing metacognitive awareness. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 19(4), 460–475.**: Introduces the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory (MAI), operationalising the knowledge-of-cognition and regulation-of-cognition distinction for educational assessment. Important for understanding how the Nelson–Narrens framework has been translated into practical measurement.

- **Fleming, S. M. (2021). *Know Thyself: The Science of Self-Awareness*. Basic Books.**: A comprehensive, accessible synthesis of metacognition research from the neuroscientific perspective. Provides an excellent overview of how the Nelson–Narrens framework connects to neural substrates, signal detection approaches, and computational models.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of Reading Research*. New York: Longman.**: Foundational treatment of comprehension monitoring and fix-up strategies during reading. Directly supports the analysis in Section III of monitoring mechanisms and their failure modes.

- **Bjork, R. A., Dunlosky, J., & Kornell, N. (2013). Self-regulated learning: Beliefs, techniques, and illusions. *Annual Review of Psychology*, 64, 417-444.**: Comprehensive review of metacognitive illusions — fluency illusion, overconfidence, desirable difficulties — that informed the calibration analysis in Section V. Key source for the argument that subjective learning signals are systematically misleading.

- **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. E. Weinert & R. H. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, Motivation, and Understanding* (pp. 65-116). Hillsdale, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum.**: Established the knowledge-of-cognition vs. regulation-of-cognition distinction that structures the entire report. Essential reading for understanding the dual nature of metacognition.

- **Clark, A., & Chalmers, D. J. (1998). The extended mind. *Analysis*, 58(1), 7-19.**: The foundational paper for the extended mind thesis, providing the philosophical framework for the Extended Metacognitive Architecture Model (EMAM) developed in Section VI. The parity principle is the key argumentative tool.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906-911.**: The seminal paper introducing metacognition as a research programme. Establishes the taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy) and introduces the concept of metacognitive experiences. Starting point for Section I.

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Unskilled and unaware of it: How difficulties in recognizing one's own incompetence lead to inflated self-assessments. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 77(6), 1121-1134.**: Original paper on the Dunning-Kruger effect. Supports the calibration analysis in Section V regarding the structural bootstrapping problem of metacognitive assessment.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. H. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125-173). New York: Academic Press.**: The paper that established the meta-level/object-level model of metacognitive monitoring and control. Foundational for the monitoring-control architecture analysed throughout the report.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science*, 17(3), 249-255.**: Influential demonstration of the testing effect. Supports the argument in Sections III and V that retrieval practice provides more accurate metacognitive monitoring signals than re-reading.

- **Schraw, G. (1998). Promoting general metacognitive awareness. *Instructional Science*, 26, 113-125.**: Synthesised the tripartite model of metacognitive knowledge (declarative, procedural, conditional) used in Section I. The conditional knowledge component is particularly important for the report's analysis of strategy selection in PKM contexts.

- **Sparrow, B., Liu, J., & Wegner, D. M. (2011). Google effects on memory: Cognitive consequences of having information at our fingertips. *Science*, 333(6043), 776-778.**: Empirical evidence for the cognitive offloading effect discussed in Section V. Demonstrates that knowledge of external storage availability reduces internal encoding effort, supporting the PKM calibration paradox analysis.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13-39). San Diego: Academic Press.**: The foundational articulation of the three-phase SRL cycle (forethought → performance → self-reflection) that provides the structural framework for the PKM-SRL isomorphism analysed in Section IV.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451-502). San Diego: Academic Press.**: Develops the four-column SRL framework integrating cognitive, motivational, behavioural, and contextual regulation. Supports the report's acknowledgment of motivational dimensions discussed in Section IV and the limitations section.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist, 34*(10), 906–911.**: The foundational theoretical statement of metacognitive development theory. Flavell introduces the term metacognition, distinguishes metacognitive knowledge from metacognitive experiences and from metacognitive monitoring/regulation, and outlines a developmental research agenda. Essential reading for understanding the conceptual vocabulary of the field. Particularly relevant to Section 1 of this report.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation, 26*, 125–173.**: The formalization of the monitoring-control framework that structures most contemporary metacognitive research. Nelson and Narens introduce the meta-level/object-level distinction, define monitoring as upward information flow and control as downward information flow, and provide evidence for the functional independence of these two processes. Central to Sections 3 and 4 of this report.

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Important for its documentation of the co-developmental constraint in action. Read alongside subsequent critiques of the original statistical analysis (Nuhfer et al., 2017; Gignac & Zajenkowski, 2020) for a more nuanced picture.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction, 1*(2), 117–175.**: The original empirical demonstration of the reciprocal teaching model for reading comprehension and metacognitive strategy development. Documents large effect sizes for comprehension improvement in initially poor readers, providing the strongest direct evidence for the efficacy of metacognitive strategy instruction when properly embedded in authentic content contexts. Central to Section 8.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The clearest theoretical statement of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model, integrating forethought, performance, and self-reflection phases. Essential for understanding the relationship between metacognition and broader SRL frameworks. Relevant to Section 6.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 452–502). Academic Press.**: Pintrich's comprehensive theoretical statement of motivational-metacognitive integration within the SRL model. Particularly valuable for the account of how goal orientation (mastery vs. performance approach vs. performance avoidance) shapes metacognitive monitoring signal interpretation. Relevant to Sections 6 and 7.

- **Ericsson, K. A., Krampe, R. T., & Tesch-Römer, C. (1993). The role of deliberate practice in the acquisition of expert performance. *Psychological Review, 100*(3), 363–406.**: The foundational paper establishing deliberate practice theory; places metacognitive monitoring of performance against accurate feedback and work at the RPL as the central mechanism distinguishing expert-producing practice from mere experience accumulation. - **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. E. Weinert & R. H. Lawrence Erlbaum.**: Brown's comprehensive theoretical synthesis integrating metacognition, executive function, and self-regulation. Addresses the developmental trajectory across childhood and proposes a domain-general–domain-specific resolution of the transfer paradox. Relevant to Sections 2 and 8.

- **Metcalfe, J., & Finn, B. (2008). ## Methodology Notes

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Research Grounding for This Report** *(from [[self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06]])*
> This focused analysis draws on five decades of cognitive educational psychology, integrating theoretical frameworks (Zimmerman, Pintrich, Flavell) with empirical evidence from laboratory studies, meta-analyses, and intervention research. The theoretical claims about the cyclical model, goal orientation effects, and self-efficacy dynamics are well-established — supported by multiple independent research groups across cultural and age-group boundaries. The mechanistic analysis in Phase IV (particularly the monitoring-control coupling framework and the recency bias in efficacy updating)…

---

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Research Grounding for This Report** *(from [[metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20]])*
> This report draws on three types of claims, which the reader should distinguish. First, *empirically established claims*: the fluency illusion, monitoring gap, and Dunning-Kruger findings are well-replicated and represent the settled empirical landscape; citations are provided. Second, *theoretical integrations*: the three-mechanism model (cue invalidity, threshold insensitivity, regulatory poverty) is an original analytical synthesis of the empirical literature; it is grounded in the evidence but goes beyond any single study or theoretical tradition. Third, *original analytical contributions*: the infrastructure reconceptualization, the phenomenological substitution formulation, and the FAR transfer structural analogues represent Claude's analytical contributions, clearly flagged throughout as such. The FAR transfer section draws on structural mapping theory (Gentner, 1983) and transfer of learning research (Perkins & Salomon, 1992) as its methodological grounding; the analogues are structural, not surface-level, isomorphisms verified against the abstract principles extracted from the original analysis.

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Research Grounding for This Report** *(from [[metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20]])*
> This report integrates evidence from empirical studies in educational psychology, cognitive psychology, and instructional design. Empirically established claims (the fluency illusion, the effectiveness advantage of adaptive over fixed scaffolding, the expertise reversal effect) draw on published experimental research identified in the references above. Theoretical integrations (the monitoring-control analysis applied to scaffolding design, the three-mechanism account of effective scaffolding) represent the report's synthesis of multiple research traditions. Claude's original analytical contributions (the "comfort trap" framing, the cognitive prosthetic synthesis, the design triangle, the externalised prefrontal function synthesis) are explicitly identified with the `[!original-synthesis]` and `[!analytical-insight]` callouts throughout. The FAR Transfer phase draws on the [[Structure-Mapping-Theory|structural mapping theory]] tradition (Gentner, 1983) and the [[Transfer-of-Learning]] literature. No fabricated references are included.

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Research Grounding for This Report** *(from [[zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Traditions Synthesized:** Social cognitive learning theory (Bandura, Zimmerman), metacognitive research (Flavell, Winne, Nelson-Narens), attribution theory (Weiner, Dweck), formative assessment research (Black & Wiliam), and organizational learning theory (Argyris & Schön). The FAR transfer phase draws on control engineering and deliberate practice theory as structural analogues.
> 
> **Claim-Type Taxonomy:**
> 
> | Claim Type | Epistemic Status | Basis |
> |-----------|-----------------|-------|
> | Three-phase structure with sub-processes | Well-established | Zimmerman (2000, 2002) + extensive replication |
> | Phase-transition mechanism (self-reflection→forethought) | Established | Attribution retraining studies, self-efficacy updating research, expert-novice comparisons |
> | Monitoring-control decoupling as primary failure mode | Moderate-Established | Monitoring research literature; see Monitoring-Control-Coupling |
> | Degenerate cycle construct | Original-Provisional | Developed in this report to explain generalization failure; requires empirical operationalization |
> | Calibration engine framing | Original-Provisional | Novel synthesis framing; explanatory but not yet empirically tested as a distinct construct |
> | FAR transfer structural analogues | Structural Argument | Conceptual; the analogues are argued on structural grounds, not empirical identity |
> 
> **Established vs. Original:** The three-phase structure, the phase-transition mechanism, the attribution-to-self-efficacy updating pathway, and the expert-novice self-reflection differences are all well-established in the literature. The *degenerate cycle* construct, the *calibration engine* framing, and the precise identification of monitoring-control decoupling as a *transition failure* (rather than a within-phase failure) are this report's original analytical contributions. These contributions are clearly marked throughout using the Analytical Commentary System.
> 
> **Limitations:** This report does not cover the full SRL literature and does not provide a comprehensive treatment of competing models (Pintrich's, Boekaerts's). The temporal boundary conditions of the cyclical model are flagged as a significant gap. The report's evidence base relies heavily on Zimmerman's own research program; independent programmatic replications of the cycle's transition mechanism in complex real-world learning contexts are limited.
>

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Research Grounding for This Report** *(from [[pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22]])*
> This report draws on (1) empirically established claims: the self-efficacy-to-strategy prediction relationship, the mastery-orientation-to-deep-processing relationship, the functional differentiation of task value and self-efficacy as distinct motivational pathways, and the factor structure of the MSLQ as established across multiple validation studies; (2) theoretical integrations: the treatment of the forethought phase as a pre-architectural construction, the monitoring-bandwidth problem, and the comparison of Pintrich's and Zimmerman's architectural differences — these are theoretically motivated claims consistent with the evidence but not directly tested by any single study; (3) Claude's original analytical contributions: the Motivational-Regulatory Asymmetry framing in Phase VI, the self-efficacy-as-sensitivity-adjustment account of the monitoring-control coupling in Phase VI, the monitoring-bandwidth problem articulation in Phase IV, and the three FAR Transfer principles with their structural analogues in Phase VII. The attribution claim in (3) is flagged because these syntheses go beyond what any individual source states, though they are grounded in and consistent with the reviewed evidence. The FAR Transfer methodology is grounded in Gentner's structural mapping theory and Barnett & Ceci's (2002) taxonomy of transfer dimensions.

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Epistemic Transparency Statement** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Traditions and Disciplines Synthesised:**
> This report synthesises findings and frameworks from cognitive psychology (metacognition research), educational psychology (self-regulated learning), philosophy of mind (extended cognition), information science (personal knowledge management), and instructional design (scaffolding, cognitive load theory). The integration is inherently interpretive — connecting findings from these distinct traditions requires argumentative bridges that go beyond what any single tradition has established.
> 
> **Claim Type Taxonomy:**
> 
> | Claim Type | Epistemic Status | Examples in This Report |
> |------------|-----------------|------------------------|
> | Framework descriptions (Flavell, Brown, Nelson-Narens, Zimmerman) | **Established** — these are widely accepted models in their fields | Sections I, IV |
> | Empirical findings (fluency illusion, testing effect, Dunning-Kruger) | **Established** — peer-reviewed, replicated findings | Sections III, V |
> | Cross-framework correspondences (PKM-SRL isomorphism, monitoring-externalisation) | **Well-motivated** — logically argued but interpretive | Sections II, III, IV |
> | Theoretical integrations (EMAM, Metacognitive PKM Lifecycle) | **Speculative** — original to this report, theoretically motivated but empirically untested | Sections VI, VII |
> | Design prescriptions (calibration-aware review, scaffold fading) | **Provisional** — derived from established principles but context-dependent | Sections V, VII |
> 
> **Distinction Between Established Findings and Original Contributions:**
> The empirical findings reported (metacognitive monitoring accuracy, fluency illusions, testing effects, SRL cycles) are well-established in the research literature. The two original frameworks — the **Extended Metacognitive Architecture Model (EMAM)** and the **Metacognitive PKM Lifecycle** — are theoretical syntheses that organise existing findings into new integrative structures. They are offered as well-motivated conceptual frameworks, not as empirically validated theories. Both generate testable hypotheses.
> 
> **Explicit Limitations:**
> 1. The report relies primarily on structural analogy between metacognitive research (conducted in laboratory and classroom settings) and PKM practice (occurring in real-world knowledge work). Direct empirical studies of metacognitive outcomes in PKM contexts are scarce.
> 2. The philosophical arguments about extended metacognition (Section VI) are conceptual, not empirical. They depend on accepting the parity principle, which is itself debated in philosophy of mind.
> 3. Motivational and affective dimensions of the metacognition-PKM relationship receive less attention than they warrant.
> 4. Individual differences in metacognitive capacity and their implications for PKM design are not treated in depth.
>

> [!methodology-and-sources] **Methodology and Epistemic Transparency** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Traditions synthesised:** This report draws on cognitive psychology (the experimental metamemory tradition of Nelson, Dunlosky, and Koriat), developmental psychology (Flavell's metacognitive taxonomy), educational psychology (the SRL frameworks of Zimmerman, Pintrich, Winne, and Schraw), cognitive neuroscience (Fleming's neural correlates programme), and computational cognitive science (predictive processing and Bayesian approaches).
> 
> **Claim type taxonomy:**
> 
> | Claim Type | Epistemic Status | Example |
> |------------|-----------------|---------|
> | Model architecture (meta-level/object-level, two channels) | Established — canonical formulation by Nelson & Narrens (1990) | "Metacognition is best understood as a structured informational relationship between two levels" |
> | Empirical findings (delayed JOL effect, FOK validity) | Established — replicated across multiple laboratories and paradigms | "Delayed JOLs are dramatically more accurate than immediate JOLs" |
> | Cue utilisation mechanisms | Established — Koriat (1997) and subsequent replications | "Metacognitive judgments are based on heuristic cues rather than direct access" |
> | SRL framework integrations | Well-motivated (interpretive) — widely accepted in educational psychology | "The Nelson–Narrens model functions as the cognitive core of SRL frameworks" |
> | Neural substrate claims | Established — Fleming & Lau (2014) and subsequent neuroimaging/lesion studies | "Anterior prefrontal cortex is selectively involved in metacognitive monitoring" |
> | Calibration–Regulation Coupling Thesis | Speculative (original to this report) — well-motivated synthesis requiring empirical validation | "Calibration and resolution map differentially onto threshold and allocation control" |
> | Three-Gradient Monitoring Failure Taxonomy | Speculative (original to this report) — organising framework requiring validation | "Monitoring failures divide into cue-validity, cue-availability, and model-integration failures" |
> | Predictive processing parallels | Provisional — structural analogy, not empirically established equivalence | "The monitoring–control architecture may be an instance of a hierarchical predictive processing motif" |
> 
> **Distinction between established findings and original contributions:** This report's primary mode is analytical exposition of established research. Two original contributions are explicitly marked with `[!original-synthesis]` callouts: the Calibration–Regulation Coupling Thesis (Section VI) and the Three-Gradient Monitoring Failure Taxonomy (Section VI). These are presented as well-motivated theoretical proposals, not established findings.
> 
> **Limitations:** This report synthesises published literature through the lens of a single architectural model. Alternative frameworks (e.g., Proust's purely functional approach to metacognition, Arango-Muñoz's action-based account) receive less attention than they deserve. The empirical literature reviewed skews toward the paired-associate metamemory paradigm; metacognition in reasoning, perception, and motor cognition is addressed but not treated with the same depth.
> 
> **AI generation transparency:** This report was generated by Claude (Anthropic) in collaboration with a human researcher. The synthesis, selection of sources, and original contributions reflect Claude's analytical processing of published literature. All citations refer to real publications; none have been fabricated. The Calibration–Regulation Coupling Thesis and Three-Gradient Monitoring Failure Taxonomy are original to this report and have not been previously published.
